This 5-minute-per-call delay is designed to outlast automated sandboxes, which typically give samples 2-5 minutes of execution time. One sandbox vendor recorded an 8-minute total wait before any malicious behavior appeared.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eKilling your antivirus before it can fight back\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe most operationally significant component is the WDAC Code Integrity policy.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWindows Defender Application Control (WDAC) policies tell the operating system which executables are allowed to run. The attackers deploy a deny-list policy to \u003ccode\u003e%WINDIR%\\System32\\CodeIntegrity\\SIPolicy.p7b\u003c/code\u003e that blocks 50 executable paths across six security vendors plus core Windows security services:\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003eTarget\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003eBlocked executables\u003c/th\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/thead\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eAvast\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eAvastSvc.exe, AvastUI.exe, afwServ.exe, aswEngSrv.exe, aswidsagent.exe, aswToolsSvc.exe, wsc_proxy.exe, Icarus components, Overseer\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eAVG\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eAll executables under Program Files\\AVG\\Antivirus\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eAvira\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eSecurity, VPN, Fallback Updater, Optimizer Host, Endpoint Protection SDK\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eWindows Defender\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eMsMpEng.exe, MpCmdRun.exe, MpDefenderCoreService.exe\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eWindows Security\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eSecurityHealthHost.exe, SecurityHealthService.exe, SecurityHealthSystray.exe\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eWindows Update\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eMoUsoCoreWorker.exe, wuauclt.exe, wusa.exe\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eOthers\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eHitmanPro, Kaspersky Virus Removal Tool, Farbar Recovery Scan Tool\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003eAfter the next reboot, none of these executables can start. The AV service is effectively dead. It won't run, it can't scan, and Windows Update can't push signature updates. One victim in the Reddit thread reported that Windows Defender disappeared entirely after running the trojanized installer.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is a known but uncommon TTP (MITRE T1562.001). It requires the installer to run elevated, which the SmartScreen bypass dialog would have prompted for. Users who clicked through both warnings — SmartScreen and UAC — gave the malware everything it needed.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHow the dropper provisions the bot\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eStage 2's strings are all XOR-obfuscated with the same \u003ccode\u003efywo\u003c/code\u003e key used for its resources. After deobfuscation, we recovered 122 strings including registry paths, API names, and encrypted configuration values.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe dropper writes the bot's initial configuration to \u003ccode\u003eHKCU\\SOFTWARE\\Python\u003c/code\u003e. Both the registry value names and data are RC4-encrypted and hex-encoded — making the registry entries look like random hex strings to casual inspection. We recovered two configuration values:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e15e9100cccadef17d91598e244b0b038\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eCampaignID\u003c/strong\u003e a unique identifier for this campaign, used during bot registration with the C2 server.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e7a2666057614636498fba93e483028cf\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eDDRList\u003c/strong\u003e six dead-drop resolver URLs that the bot uses to discover its C2 servers (detailed in the next section).\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eNotably, the dropper does not write a C2 server list directly. The bot discovers its command-and-control addresses exclusively through the dead-drop resolver mechanism — a design choice that makes the infrastructure resilient to takedowns.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eDead-drop resolvers: how the bot finds its C2\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eA dead-drop resolver (DDR) is an intermediary, a legitimate web page where the operator publishes encrypted C2 addresses. The bot fetches the page, extracts and decrypts the content, and connects to whatever C2 servers it finds. If the operator needs to rotate infrastructure, they update the DDR page and every infected machine follows automatically.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe bot reads its DDR list from the registry, fetches each URL, and RC4-decrypts the page content. Valid payloads start with the prefix \u003ccode\u003eVAL:\u003c/code\u003e followed by a semicolon-separated list of C2 URLs.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe extracted all six dead-drop resolver URLs from the dropper and probed them:\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003eURL\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003eStatus\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003eDecrypted content\u003c/th\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/thead\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003etelegra[.]ph/tr02-05-02\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eLive\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eC2 list: parkspringshotel[.]com + auraguest[.]lk\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003etelegra[.]ph/ki06-05-02\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eLive\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003eVAL:XX\u003c/code\u003e — placeholder for future C2 rotation\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003erentry[.]co/zzk7opdu\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eLive\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eSame C2 list\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003erentry[.]org/wayc2qqz\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eLive\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003eVAL:XX\u003c/code\u003e — placeholder\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003ecodeberg[.]org/etienne74/guix/...\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e404\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eRemoved or never created\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e`2wxjoz6lkdxkniksujbdknkugweemx7uvxdr4ejgyipce4tnvktvbvqd.onion/t`\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eNot probed\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eTor hidden service DDR\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe operator has two live C2 servers and four reserve dead-drop pages — two stubs ready for C2 rotation and one Tor fallback. If the current C2 infrastructure gets burned, the operator updates a Telegraph or Rentry page and every infected machine picks up the new address on its next poll cycle.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBeyond the dead-drop mechanism, the bot has a domain generation algorithm (DGA) as a last-resort fallback. It combines four seeds (\u003ccode\u003eXNerWs4I\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003ee55gegnW\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003eRjWN0Y9m\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003eAemOU1co\u003c/code\u003e) with 14 TLDs and an ISO-week-based rotation to produce 56 candidate domains per week. We probed all current DGA domains — none are registered. The operator is running purely on dead-drop infrastructure for now, but the DGA ensures the botnet can survive even if all resolver pages and C2 servers are taken down simultaneously.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe C2 protocol: RSA-OAEP wrapping AES-GCM\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe reversed the bot's full communication protocol from the PyArmor-protected bytecode. It uses a layered encryption scheme designed to prevent passive interception and session tampering:\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-05/ChatGPT%20Image%20May%2011%2C%202026%2C%2008_27_38%20AM.png.webp?itok=SAUeCTUe 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-05/ChatGPT%20Image%20May%2011%2C%202026%2C%2008_27_38%20AM.png.webp?itok=mYiY5fc- 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":967},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-05/ChatGPT%20Image%20May%2011%2C%202026%2C%2008_27_38%20AM.png.webp?itok=M23-fx2A 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-05/ChatGPT%20Image%20May%2011%2C%202026%2C%2008_27_38%20AM.png.webp?itok=D03z7_6R 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":735},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-05/ChatGPT%20Image%20May%2011%2C%202026%2C%2008_27_38%20AM.png.webp?itok=J_oCQFhq 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-05/ChatGPT%20Image%20May%2011%2C%202026%2C%2008_27_38%20AM.png.webp?itok=f2a4U07U 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":570}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-05/ChatGPT%20Image%20May%2011%2C%202026%2C%2008_27_38%20AM.png.webp?itok=J_oCQFhq","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eThe bot wraps a fresh AES-256 key with the hardcoded RSA-2048 public key (OAEP, SHA-256) and sends it to the C2. The server proves possession of the private key by returning \u003ccode\u003e\"ok\"\u003c/code\u003e encrypted with AES-GCM. From that point, every message in both directions is AES-256-GCM encrypted with a per-direction sequence counter as authenticated associated data — preventing replay and reordering.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAfter the handshake, the bot registers with the campaign ID provisioned by the dropper. The server returns a \u003ccode\u003ebot_id\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003ebot_pass\u003c/code\u003e that the bot stores in the registry for subsequent polling. The bot's primary capability is arbitrary Python code execution — the attacker can ship any Python code and the bot will run it and return the output.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAll identified C2 and staging servers are compromised \u003ca href=\"https://qloapps.com/\"\u003eQloApps\u003c/a\u003e hotel reservation websites:\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003eDomain\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003eRole\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003eIP\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003ePHP\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003eLocation\u003c/th\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/thead\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003eparkspringshotel[.]com\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eC2 panel\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e172.96.172[.]91\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e8.3.30\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eGhana\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003eauraguest[.]lk\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eC2 panel\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e209.133.215[.]178\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e8.3.30\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eSri Lanka\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003echeckinnhotels[.]com\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eLinux payload staging\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e66.29.137[.]25\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e7.4.33\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e—\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe two C2 servers share identical JARM fingerprints and run the same PHP version, confirming the same C2 panel software is deployed on both. The third domain, \u003ccode\u003echeckinnhotels[.]com\u003c/code\u003e, was used to host the Linux payload disguised as an SVG file (\u003ca href=\"https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/jdownloader-site-hacked-to-replace-installers-with-python-rat-malware/\"\u003eBleepingComputer\u003c/a\u003e). All three run QloApps — an open-source hotel booking CMS — suggesting the attacker specifically targets this software to build their infrastructure. None of the domains had malicious detections on VirusTotal at the time of discovery. We confirmed both C2 servers remain operationally live as of May 11.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe r77 rootkit stager\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe rootkit stager is a 176 KB native x86 PE based on the open-source \u003ca href=\"https://github.com/bytecode77/r77-rootkit\"\u003er77 rootkit\u003c/a\u003e. It contains an embedded .NET assembly that performs four operations:\u003c/p\u003e\u003col\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e9ca3772c2bd6052d86931268e23d036a\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eAMSI bypass\u003c/strong\u003e — a PowerShell cradle patches \u003ccode\u003eAmsiScanBuffer\u003c/code\u003e in memory, disabling Windows' Antimalware Scan Interface before any script payload runs.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e01c27cd4c721b2dabe18b819d05c84fe\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eReflective DLL injection\u003c/strong\u003e — injects the r77 hooking DLLs (32-bit and 64-bit variants) into \u003ccode\u003ewinlogon.exe\u003c/code\u003e. These DLLs hook Windows API functions to hide any process, file, or registry key whose name starts with the \u003ccode\u003e$77\u003c/code\u003e prefix.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e254d7da7ebd6dfd7d57f7c5f67760ba3\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eRegistry persistence\u003c/strong\u003e — stores the stager assembly as a binary blob in \u003ccode\u003eHKLM\\SOFTWARE\\$77stager\u003c/code\u003e and reloads it at every boot via .NET reflection.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ef200c38559748bd535edaf6a0d5aa3d5\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eService creation\u003c/strong\u003e — installs a Windows service (\u003ccode\u003e$77svc\u003c/code\u003e) to ensure the rootkit survives reboots independently of the registry mechanism.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003cp\u003eOnce r77 is active, the \u003ccode\u003e$77\u003c/code\u003e prefix acts as an invisibility cloak. The Python bot calls \u003ccode\u003er77_hide_self()\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003er77_hide_registry_path()\u003c/code\u003e to rename its own process and registry hive with this prefix, making them invisible to Task Manager, \u003ccode\u003eregedit\u003c/code\u003e, and most security tools.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe Python bot under PyArmor v9\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe final payload is a Python script protected by \u003ca href=\"https://pyarmor.dashingsoft.com/\"\u003ePyArmor\u003c/a\u003e v9, a commercial bytecode obfuscation tool. PyArmor encrypts each function's bytecode individually and decrypts it at runtime through a native DLL — making static analysis significantly harder than standard Python malware.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe built a custom tool to decrypt and disassemble the protected bytecode, recovering 30 functions totaling 7,326 lines of disassembly. The bot is cross-platform (Windows and Linux), uses gevent for asynchronous I/O, and handles the full lifecycle: configuration management, C2 discovery via dead-drop resolvers and DGA, encrypted communication, task execution, and rootkit integration.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe bot's primary capability is executing arbitrary Python code received from the C2 server. Combined with the Python 3.14 runtime installed by the dropper, this gives the operator the ability to deploy any follow-on payload — keyloggers, credential stealers, lateral movement tools — without needing to push new binaries to the victim.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch2\u003eImpact and recommendations\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eUsers who downloaded JDownloader from the official website between May 6 and May 7, 2026, using the \"Download Alternative Installer\" links (Windows) or the Linux shell installer link are potentially compromised. The \u003ca href=\"https://jdownloader.org/incident_8.5.2026.html\"\u003eofficial incident report\u003c/a\u003e lists eight malicious installer hashes across x86/amd64 and multiple JRE versions. The attackers exploited an unpatched CMS vulnerability to swap download links — the actual installer files hosted externally were never modified. In-app updates, macOS downloads, and package manager installs (Flatpak, Winget, Snap) were not affected.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe Linux attack chain, [first reported by BleepingComputer](https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/jdownloader-site-hacked-to-replace-installers-with-python-rat-malware/), follows a different delivery path. We downloaded the trojanized shell installer and its payload to verify the claims and dig deeper.d. The modified install4j script requires root, checks for x86_64 architecture, and downloads a 20 MB tar.gz from \u003ccode\u003echeckinnhotels[.]com/img/logo1.svg\u003c/code\u003e (disguised with \u003ccode\u003eContent-Type: image/svg+xml\u003c/code\u003e — still live as of May 11). The archive contains two files: a 3 KB SUID-root helper (\u003ccode\u003esystemd-exec\u003c/code\u003e) and a 21 MB PyInstaller bundle (\u003ccode\u003epkg\u003c/code\u003e). The helper is a minimal ELF that calls \u003ccode\u003esetuid(0)\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003esetgid(0)\u003c/code\u003e, and \u003ccode\u003eexecvp()\u003c/code\u003e — installed to \u003ccode\u003e/usr/bin/systemd-exec\u003c/code\u003e with \u003ccode\u003echmod 4755\u003c/code\u003e. Persistence is created via \u003ccode\u003e/etc/profile.d/systemd.sh\u003c/code\u003e, which launches the payload masquerading as \u003ccode\u003e/usr/libexec/upowerd\u003c/code\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe decrypted the PyArmor-protected Python bytecode from the Linux payload and confirmed it is \u003cstrong\u003ethe same bot source code\u003c/strong\u003e as the Windows variant — identical RSA public key, DGA seeds, RC4 keys, C2 protocol, and DDR handler logic. The same campaign ID (\u003ccode\u003eNdcHsJPM\u003c/code\u003e) and all six DDR URLs are written to \u003ccode\u003e~/.local/share/\u003c/code\u003e as RC4-encrypted files (mirroring the Windows registry approach). The only difference is the Python runtime: the Linux bundle uses Python 3.12 (packaged via PyInstaller) versus Python 3.14 on Windows.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe JDownloader developers restored clean downloads on May 9 and published a detailed incident timeline. Users who already had JDownloader installed and received updates through the application's built-in updater were not affected — only fresh downloads from the website during the compromise window were trojanized.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eIf you ran the trojanized installer:\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e78c8637f87f47adb2a6c0e9e37ff1466\"\u003eThe WDAC policy may have disabled your antivirus. Check \u003ccode\u003e%WINDIR%\\System32\\CodeIntegrity\\SIPolicy.p7b\u003c/code\u003e — if it exists and you didn't create it, delete it and reboot.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e5a9344602452a9e77dfe81e30c45fd8c\"\u003eThe registry keys \u003ccode\u003eHKCU\\SOFTWARE\\Python\u003c/code\u003e (bot config) and \u003ccode\u003eHKLM\\SOFTWARE\\$77stager\u003c/code\u003e (rootkit) are strong indicators of compromise — but if r77 is active, its API hooks hide all \u003ccode\u003e$77\u003c/code\u003e-prefixed registry keys and the bot's own hive from standard tools like \u003ccode\u003eregedit\u003c/code\u003e. To inspect these, boot from a Windows recovery environment or mount the registry hives offline.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"edcbc2f271c7b2885893f8f995fe252eb\"\u003eA clean OS reinstall is the safest remediation. The r77 rootkit's reflective injection into \u003ccode\u003ewinlogon.exe\u003c/code\u003e and AMSI bypass make partial cleanup unreliable.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eFor defenders:\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"efcd7ce434d65f159da0dbff097d5fb44\"\u003eThe bot uses the default Python User-Agent \u003ccode\u003ePython-urllib/3.14\u003c/code\u003e. Registry\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eT1112\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor customers using Norton, Avast, AVG, Avira or any other security product, the most important question is whether the malicious installer was executed. If you downloaded an affected installer but did not run it, delete the file, download a fresh installer from the official JDownloader site, and run a full scan with up-to-date protection. If you ran the installer, or cannot rule out that you ran it, treat the system as compromised. JDownloader recommends a clean operating system reinstall in this situation, and we agree that this is the safest baseline. The Windows payload includes rootkit behavior, persistence, encrypted configuration and policy-based defense evasion, so a normal scan may reduce risk but cannot guarantee that every persistence mechanism has been removed. Until the system has been rebuilt or inspected from a trusted recovery environment, avoid using it for sensitive activity. Do not log in to email, banking, work accounts, password managers or other important services from that machine.
